## The Core math block

| Id | Date | Topic | Decision | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D-641 | 2026-09-18 | The rounding rule of fixed-point math (OQ-60) | Core rounds toward zero. The C# integer division operator already rounds this way, so the round itself needs no helper. The fixed-point type holds the one multiply and the one divide, and every system calls them. A system that must never give nothing sets its own floor, such as a minimum of 1 damage. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-60. Applies D-169, G-2, and T-1. The rule is symmetric about zero, and a long chain of steps drifts toward zero. A round toward negative infinity and a round to the nearest integer were the other options, and each one needs a helper on every operation. PR-4 writes the type and its test vectors. |
| D-642 | 2026-09-18 | The random generator of Core (OQ-61) | PCG32, the pcg-xsh-rr 64/32 variant: a 64-bit state and a 32-bit output. The reference C code at `https://www.pcg-random.org/` publishes the test output, and PR-4 commits those vectors. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves the first half of OQ-61. Applies G-3, G-4, and T-7. The generator uses integer math alone, so `det-lint` passes it (G-2). The sequence constant of PCG32 carries the stream number of D-643. A 64-bit draw takes two 32-bit draws. xoshiro256** and SplitMix64 were the other options, and neither one publishes a canonical vector file. |
| D-643 | 2026-09-18 | The stream split (OQ-61) | Each stream takes its seed from the run seed and a fixed stream number, with no order between the streams. A new subsystem never moves the numbers of an existing stream. The hash function of D-644 makes the seed. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves the second half of OQ-61. Applies G-4, F-35, and D-259: the snapshot holds the position of every stream. A master generator that seeds each stream in order was the other option, and a new stream moves the seed of every stream after it. PR-4 writes the split and a test that proves two streams of one seed give different sequences. |
| D-644 | 2026-09-18 | The hash functions of Core (OQ-62) | Core holds two hash functions. xxHash64 makes the state hash and the seed of each stream. A SHA-256 in Core code makes the content hash. The owner chose the second option, and the session recommended one function for every hash. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-62. Applies F-35, G-1, G-5, and D-494: Core can check the content hash of a record with no other project. The content hash then resists a content file that somebody builds to collide. Each function ships with the published test vectors of its reference implementation. The cost is two functions in Core against T-1. PR-4 writes xxHash64, and PR-5 writes SHA-256 with the content hash that calls it. |
| D-645 | 2026-09-18 | The PR that writes the SHA-256 | PR-5 writes the SHA-256 of D-644, beside the content hash that calls it. PR-4 writes xxHash64 alone, because the state hash and the stream seeds call it. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Applies D-644, G-8, and T-1: no PR ships code that nothing calls. Sections 7.11 and 7.12 of `docs/roadmaps/phase-1-foundations.md` and section 7.7 of `docs/roadmaps/area-core.md` move the SHA-256 line to PR-5. OQ-62 closes with D-644, and the move changes no answer. |

## The content block

| Id | Date | Topic | Decision | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D-646 | 2026-09-18 | The form of a content id (OQ-63) | A content id is a lowercase kind, a dot, and a lowercase name, such as `enemy.cave_rat` or `item.rusted_key`. The form is `^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*\.[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$`. The kind of an id agrees with the file that holds the entry. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-63. Applies D-166, D-493, D-519, T-1, and T-2. An id in a save, a record, a log, or an art file names its kind, so no reader needs a second field. PR-5 writes three tests: the form of each id, the agreement of a kind with its file, and no repeated id across every rule file. A bare name per kind, a bare name in one namespace, and a number were the other options. A thing that moves to another kind takes a new id, because an id is permanent. |
| D-647 | 2026-09-18 | The no-reflection rule of the content reader (OQ-179) | Core reads content with a hand reader on `Utf8JsonReader`, and Core calls `JsonSerializer` nowhere. `Directory.Build.props` also sets `JsonSerializerIsReflectionEnabledByDefault` to `false`, so each program of the solution carries the switch. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-179. Answers F-36, and applies D-116, D-177, G-2, G-6, and T-2. Exit tests 1 to 3 of section 7.12 of `docs/roadmaps/phase-1-foundations.md` need the file and the field in each error, and the refusal of a fraction or an exponent. A hand reader gives all three from one place, and `JsonSerializer` needs a wrapper and a converter for the same result. No reflection path exists in Core, because no call exists. PR-5 adds a test that reads the switch in the test host, and `det-lint` refuses `JsonSerializer` and `System.Reflection` in Core. A hand reader with no switch, generated metadata with the switch, and the switch in each program were the other options. |
| D-648 | 2026-09-18 | The layout line of the content hash (OQ-184) | One folder, `content/rules/`, holds every file that a rule reads, and the content hash covers that folder alone. Each other folder of `content/`, such as `content/sprites/` and `content/strings/`, stays outside the hash. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-184. Applies D-495, D-501, D-519, and T-2. A reader sees the line in a path, and a move across the line shows as a move in a diff. PR-5 writes a test that proves the hash reads the files of `content/rules/` and no other file. A list of the rule folders in Core code, and a field in each file, were the other options, and each one can drop a file from the hash in silence. |
| D-649 | 2026-09-18 | The rule content of PR-5 (OQ-185) | Core holds one small fixture rule record, with a content id and a string id, and `content/rules/` holds its files. A later PR replaces the fixture with the first real rule record. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-185. Applies D-166, D-495, D-517, D-646, and T-1. The content hash, the repeated-id test, and the string-id test each read the real tree, and the reader gets a third concrete case beside the palette and the string table. The id of each fixture entry is permanent, so no later entry takes it (D-166). A record in Tests, the first real rule record, and no rule file were the other options. |

## The tick and the run record

| Id | Date | Topic | Decision | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D-650 | 2026-09-18 | The tick while a menu is open (OQ-64) | The tick count rises while a menu is open. Each step of the loop adds one to the tick, and the world systems skip their work while a menu is open. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-64. Applies D-162, D-164, D-493, G-3, and T-1. The tick is the one time line of the run, so every intent and every log line takes its order from the tick alone. The record then needs no second order field, and a replay reads no second field. A stopped tick with an order number in each menu intent was the other option, and it adds a second source of a replay mismatch. The cost is more ticks in the record, and the snapshot rule of D-651 bounds the size. |
| D-651 | 2026-09-18 | The snapshot moment of the run record (OQ-65) | The run record takes a new snapshot at each save. The record holds that snapshot and every intent after it, and a crash file holds the intents since the last save. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-65. Answers F-10, and applies D-259, D-166, and G-5. PR-6 writes the compaction rule, and PR-43 writes the save that makes each snapshot, so one snapshot code path serves both. No tick pays a snapshot cost that no save needs. A fixed count of ticks and a change of map were the other options. A fixed count costs time on a tick inside the frame budget, and a change of map gives no bound on one map. The cost is a long replay after a long play with no save, and the save points of D-224 set that distance. |
| D-652 | 2026-09-18 | The encoding of records and snapshots (OQ-66) | Records and snapshots are JSON text. A record takes one JSON object for each line, as the logs do, and a snapshot takes one JSON object. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-66. Applies D-170, D-179, D-473, D-116, G-6, T-1, and T-2. A person reads a record in a diff, in a review, and in a crash email with no tool, so triage needs no dump command. The hand reader of D-647 reads the record too, and Core calls `JsonSerializer` nowhere. A binary form and JSON with compression were the other options. Each one hides the file from a reader, and compression needs a dependency under G-13. The cost is a larger file than a binary form. |
| D-653 | 2026-09-18 | Where the game version lives (OQ-168) | One constant in Core holds the game version. The export preset and the release workflow read that constant, and a test proves that the release tag matches it. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-168. Applies D-448, G-1, G-5, and T-2. The record header, the crash file, and the title screen read one place. A build from any branch names a version, so no crash report and no record header carries an empty field. PR-6 writes the constant and the header field, and PR-31 writes the tag test and the workflow read. The git tag alone was the other option, and a build from a branch with no tag then has no version. The cost is one commit before each release tag. |
| D-654 | 2026-09-18 | The migration of a save format (OQ-186) | PR-43 ships the reader of each format version, a stored fixture save of format 1, and a test that fails a raise of the version with no fixture. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-186. Applies D-166 and D-259. `SaveFormat.Current` is 1, so one reader exists, and the reader of each older version gives the snapshot of this build. `TheThingBelow.Tests/SaveFixtureTests.cs` reads every stored save of `TheThingBelow.Tests/saves/`, and it fails a format version with no file there. A second format now, and no reader table at all, were the other options. A second format now changes the shape of the file for a test alone. The cost is a reader table with one entry until the first change of the snapshot. |
| D-655 | 2026-09-18 | The lines of a save file (OQ-187) | A save file takes two lines of JSON. Line 1 holds the format version, the simulation version, the content hash, the game version, the seed, and the checksum. Line 2 holds the snapshot. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-187. Applies D-178, D-259, and D-652. The checksum is the SHA-256 digest of the bytes of line 2, with no line ending, so one shell command checks a save. A snapshot keeps one JSON object, which D-652 sets, and line 2 of a run record keeps the same shape. A field inside the snapshot object, and a second file beside the save, were the other options. A field inside the object needs a rule for the bytes that the digest leaves out, and a reader and a writer can read that rule two ways. A second file needs two renames, and D-178 gives one. The cost is a header line that a reader passes before the snapshot. |
| D-656 | 2026-09-18 | The names of the save files (OQ-188) | The three saves live in the saves folder of the user folder of D-465, as slot.json, autosave.json, and resume.json. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-188. Applies D-62, D-258, D-465, and D-652. The crash files and the log files of PR-44 take their own folders beside the saves folder, so the user folder stays readable. The file type tells a player and a reader of a support mail what the file holds. Three files at the root of the user folder, and a file type of `.save`, were the other options. The cost is one folder level under the user folder. |
| D-657 | 2026-09-18 | The check of the save folder (OQ-189) | Game compares the user folder of Godot with the save folder of Storage at the start of every session, and it stops with both paths on a difference. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Resolves OQ-189. Applies D-465, D-481, D-494, F-33, and T-2. Godot reads the custom user folder of the project, and Storage reads the environment of the system, so two rules must give one folder. The smoke job runs the check on Windows, on Linux, and on macOS, and each leg thus proves the rule of that system (D-117). Game takes its reference to Storage here, which D-494 sets. A test of the three folders alone, and a path from Godot alone, were the other options. A test alone proves no real machine, and a path from Godot alone leaves Tools with no folder. The cost is one check in the boot path. |
| D-658 | 2026-09-18 | The folders and the names of the crash files and the log files (OQ-190) | The crash files sit in the crashes folder of the user folder, and the log files in the logs folder, beside the saves folder of D-656. A name holds the prefix of the folder, the stamp of the wall-clock time in UTC, and the `.json` type, as crash-20260918-014253.json and session-20260918-014253.json. Two files of one second take a count after the stamp. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Applies D-170, D-179, D-465, D-652, and D-656. A sort by name is a sort by time, and a second crash never replaces the report before it. One file of each kind, and a rising count, were the other options: the first destroys the report before it, and the second tells the player nothing about which file to send. The cost is a time in a name, so the host passes the time and Storage reads no clock (G-3, T-3). |
| D-659 | 2026-09-18 | How many crash files and log files the game keeps (OQ-191) | Each of the two folders keeps the newest 10 files. The game removes the older files of a folder when it makes a file in that folder. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Applies D-170, D-658, and T-2. The owner answered the removal at the start of a session. A log file opens at the start of a session, and a crash file comes later in a session, so the rule runs on each write of either folder and holds the count of both. Every file, and the newest 3, were the other options: the first grows without a bound, and the second drops the first crash of a run of crashes. The cost is one read of the folder on each write. |
| D-660 | 2026-09-18 | What a step of Core logs, and the levels (OQ-192) | The log holds four levels: debug, info, warning, and error. A menu open and a menu close log at the info level, and a beat of the patrol at the debug level. A log file holds the info level and above, and the session argument `--log-debug` adds the debug lines. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Applies D-162, D-179, D-650, T-1, and T-7. Two concrete cases prove the whole path: a step returns its entries, the filter drops a level, and Storage writes one JSON object for each line. The patrol walks two times a second, so an info file of a player holds the changes that a report follows and not every step. The menu alone, and two levels, were the other options. Each later PR that logs takes a name of `LogSubsystems` and one of the four levels. |
| D-661 | 2026-09-18 | The lines of a crash file (OQ-193) | One file holds the crash and the record. Line 1 is the crash object: the format version, the time, the type of the error, the message of each error, the stack, the simulation version, the game version, and whether a record follows. The lines of the run record follow line 1. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Applies D-170, D-448, D-473, D-652, and T-2. The player sends one file, and the reader skips line 1 and reads the rest with the record reader, so no report loses its replay. Line 1 carries the versions itself, because a crash can come before a run and the record header is then absent. Two files, a crash file and a record file, were the other option, and a player sends one file of a pair. |
| D-662 | 2026-09-18 | The simulation version of PR-44 (OQ-194) | PR-44 does not raise the simulation version, and it stays 3. A log entry never reaches the state hash, and the PR changes no rule that makes a state. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Applies G-17, D-259, and D-504. The step of Core returns its entries beside the state, as PR-43 added the text of a save beside the state. A raise to 4 was the other option, and it makes the expected hashes of the identity file and the stored fixture save wrong with no change of a rule, which hides a real rule change later. The review of this PR confirms the reading (G-17). |
| D-663 | 2026-09-18 | The CRC-32 of the PNG code (OQ-72) | Tools holds a short CRC-32 of its own, and the project takes no package for it. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Applies G-13, D-176, and D-502: no dependency, and the Effect column of D-176 already stated that rule. The class `Crc32` of `TheThingBelow.Tools/Png` holds the table of the reflected polynomial 0xedb88320, and its test reads the published check values. The package `System.IO.Hashing` was the other option: no code of our own, and a dependency with a version and a decision row. The code uses integer math alone, and det-lint reads the folder with the rules of D-502. |
| D-664 | 2026-09-18 | The row filters of the PNG code (OQ-195) | The reader restores each of the five row filters: None, Sub, Up, Average, and Paeth. The writer writes the filter None in every row. An interlaced PNG fails with the file and the reason. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Applies D-107, D-176, and T-2. Every image editor writes filtered rows, so the reader must restore each filter for the PNG import of PR-51 and for a hand edit of D-107. Apple ImageIO chose the filters Sub and Paeth for the two committed 4 by 4 files of PR-47, which proves the need. The writer stays simple, and its files are larger than the files of an encoder that picks a filter for each row. The filter None alone in both directions, and a writer that picks a filter for each row, were the other options. |
| D-665 | 2026-09-18 | The PNG fixture files of PR-47 (OQ-196) | Two small files of an outside encoder enter git: `TheThingBelow.Tests/png/rgb-4x4.png` and `rgba-4x4.png`, each under 1 KB. Each failure case is bytes that the test builds in code. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-18. Applies D-107, D-176, F-19, and T-3. The two files prove that the reader reads the files of another program, which no test of files that this project writes can prove. Resolves OQ-85. Applies D-107, D-508, D-515, and D-517. A change to one kind rewrites one page, so the git history grows slower than one page for every drawing. One page up to 8192, fixed pages of 2048 in order, and one page for each region were the other options. One page commits every drawing again on each art change. The value needs no conversion and no rounding, so two machines animate the same drawing the same way. Milliseconds, and no time in the drawing files, were the other options. A document can state which provider did a piece of work when that fact is its subject. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-207. The cost model of `docs/design.md`, the handoff, and the review records keep their provider names. The PR gate line names the list of T-6 and not the word "anywhere". |
| D-704 | 2026-09-20 | The word for the travelers in game text (OQ-208) | An in-game description says "party" for the travelers, and not "cast". The owner first picked "cast" for the five, and corrected the answer in the same session. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-208 for player text (D-11). The world documents keep their text. The id kind stays `cast` (D-698), because an id is no player text. The glossary row of "party" names the characters in battle, and a later owner answer sets the second term if the two uses collide. |
| D-705 | 2026-09-20 | The push wait of gitar (OQ-209) | A session waits three minutes after a push before it posts a `Gitar review` comment, and it posts one only when no automatic review started. The session recommended a row for this rule. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-209. The shared `gitar-review` skill held the rule with the id D-160 of another register, and D-160 of this register is the renderer test. The skill of this repository cites this row. Revised in part by D-895 on 2026-09-22, for the time of the gitar pause: no session waits for a pass or posts a `Gitar review` comment. The wait stands again at the end of the pause. D-909 ended the pause on 2026-09-23. Revised in part by D-945 on 2026-09-23, for the time of the Gitar pause: no session does the push wait or asks for a review. D-1073 ended the Gitar pause on 2026-09-24. Revised in part by D-1074 on 2026-09-24: the first read of the Gitar check comes 60 seconds after the push, and the poll runs until the check completes. The rule for the `Gitar review` comment after three minutes stands. |
| D-706 | 2026-09-20 | The protection of the administrators (OQ-200) | The protection of `main` binds an administrator too. The owner enabled the setting on 2026-09-20, in the audit session. | Owner action, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-200 and closes the gap of F-93: no direct commit reaches `main`, and D-8, D-25, and the PR gate bind every commit. |

## The UI base

| Id | Date | Topic | Decision | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D-707 | 2026-09-19 | The body size setting and its default | The player setting gives two body sizes, 24 and 32 frame pixels, and no other value. The default is 32 at a frame fit of 1x, and 24 above that fit. The title is twice the body, and no setting changes it. The owner picked the body and the title on four screens. | Owner answers, 2026-09-19, from the second run of the probe (D-621, D-629, D-638). Revises D-639 in part, and G-28 in part on the same point: the floor of 2 device pixels goes, and the two body sizes take the place of the UI values 1x and 2x. One rule and no table of screens stands, and each layout holds at both body sizes. Each body draws its own bitmap strike at 1x, so both sizes give a stem of one frame pixel. A title of 48 doubles the 24 bitmap, and a title of 64 doubles the 32 bitmap. The four picks are the Deck at a fit of 1x with a body of 32, and a 1080p screen, a 1440p screen, and a 4K screen each with a body of 24. The owner refused a shorter text limit, so D-635 stands at 76 characters. Revises D-228, D-263, and D-264 in part, the font size alone: the body draws at 24 or 32 frame pixels, and the title at 48 or 64. The family, the license, the hard pixel edge, and the 32-pixel tile stand. PR-61 builds the setting and its default. Revised in part by D-874 on 2026-09-22: the setting also stores auto, which follows the default rule. The two sizes and the rule stand. |
| D-708 | 2026-09-19 | How a panel holds text that is longer than it | A box that pages scrolls up one line on the advance intent. A panel that cannot page reflows to more rows, and it holds all of its text. The session recommended both rules. | Owner answer, 2026-09-19. Revises D-241 in part: a panel that pages holds one page, and every other panel holds its longest string at each body size of D-707. The limits of the `game-text-style` skill stand. Both rules are guardrails today, because the frame holds 77 columns at a body of 32 and the five party members need 68. Thus no panel of the two body sizes needs a reflow or a second page. |
| D-709 | 2026-09-19 | The scope and the layout of the type-out | Each prose box types one character at a time: the dialogue, a lore entry, and a map notice. The HUD, the menu labels, the button prompts, and the stat rows appear at once. A box holds its layout as it types. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-19. Confirms D-223 and D-214, and adds the scope and the layout. Closes the layout half of OQ-112. The speeds of OQ-112 and the skip of OQ-150 stay open, and both block PR-36. The box holds its layout through `VC_CHARS_AFTER_SHAPING`, which the external facts of `docs/roadmaps/area-ui-input.md` record. PR-36 builds the box, and PR-63 adds the text speed. Revised in part by D-815 on 2026-09-21: no button prompt exists. The rest stands. |
| D-710 | 2026-09-20 | The font settings and the load from bytes (OQ-104) | Game reads each font from the resources of its own assembly into `FontFile.Data`. Each font sets `Antialiasing` to `None`, `Hinting` to `None`, and `SubpixelPositioning` to `Disabled`, and it sets `MultichannelSignedDistanceField` and `GenerateMipmaps` to false. Font oversampling stays off. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20, from the probe run of 2026-09-19. Resolves OQ-104. Applies D-263, D-264, D-508, and F-49. A size with no bitmap falls back to the traced outline in silence, so Game passes the bitmap size to the rasterizer and applies the whole-number scale with a transform. Terminus TTF 4.49.3 carries bitmaps at 12, 14, 16, 18, 20, 22, 24, 28, and 32 pixels and no other size, which a read of both files on 2026-09-19 confirmed. A test reads each setting back, and a second test compares a render at 20 pixels against the 20 bitmap. |
| D-711 | 2026-09-20 | The glyph set of the last device (OQ-107) | The last key event or mouse event sets the keyboard. The last joypad event sets the gamepad. A JSON content file maps a substring of the name of the pad to a glyph set. Sony, DualSense, and DualShock give PlayStation. Steam Deck and Steam Controller give the Deck. Every other name gives Xbox. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-107. Applies D-84, D-116, D-222, D-493, G-6, and F-50: Godot reports no controller type, and a name table in content takes a new pad with no build. Game makes each intent from an input event, and never from a poll. PR-78 overrides the pick with the Steamworks call under Steam (D-460, D-553). Superseded by D-815 on 2026-09-21: no prompt draws a glyph, so no table picks a glyph set. |
| D-712 | 2026-09-20 | The crash address before the studio name (OQ-57) | The crash message carries a final string id and a placeholder address in the reserved `.invalid` top-level domain. OQ-57 stays open, and it keeps its block on PR-33 and PR-75. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-4, D-170, D-450, D-456, and D-473: the repository is public until Phase 6, so no personal address enters a file. RFC 2606 reserves `.invalid` for a name that is sure to be invalid, read 2026-09-19. The string id never changes, and the PR that closes OQ-57 changes the value alone. A build before that PR shows an address that takes no mail. |
| D-713 | 2026-09-19 | Where the fonts live | The two font files live in a fonts folder under `content/`, and the Game assembly embeds them with every other content file. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-19. Applies D-263, D-264, D-467, and D-508. No record of Core reads a font file, as no record reads a page of the atlas (D-517). `content/` already holds a binary file, so the embedded-set test of D-508 covers one. `licenses/terminus.txt` already carries the SIL Open Font License 1.1, so PR-61 adds no notice file. |
| D-714 | 2026-09-20 | The art batch of the UI base | The owner approved the 13 drawings of PR-61: the window frame of 24 by 24 pixels, and the confirm, cancel, and menu glyph of each of the four sets at 16 by 16 pixels. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-57, D-107, D-220, D-222, and G-25. The review sheets of the batch are in the description of the PR, which D-514 sets as the place. The keyboard set draws a key cap, Xbox draws a green A and a red B, PlayStation draws a cross and a circle, and the Deck draws a white A and B. Each menu glyph is a pill with three lines. Revised in part by D-815 on 2026-09-21: the 12 glyph drawings leave the content. The window frame stands. |

## The tile map and the map scene

| Id | Date | Topic | Decision | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D-715 | 2026-09-20 | The pixel snap of Game (OQ-89) | Both Godot snap settings stay off. Game rounds the camera position and each map sprite position to a whole pixel of the world viewport in its own tick code. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Closes OQ-89. Applies D-203, D-230, and D-633. A test reads the rounded value with no engine, because the rule lives in our code and not in a project setting. It agrees with F-52, where Game moves the camera from the tick. A Godot version change cannot move the rule. One helper holds the rounding, and each drawn thing of the map calls it. The world viewport of D-634 is the unit, because it holds one art pixel in one pixel. The frame shows that viewport at a scale of 2, so a whole pixel of it is a whole frame pixel too. |
| D-716 | 2026-09-20 | A diagonal step (OQ-117) | A step goes up, down, left, or right. A diagonal walk takes two steps. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Closes OQ-117. Applies D-84 and D-106. Every step costs the same count of ticks, so the record and the replay stay simple (T-7). No corner-cut rule is necessary. The patrol routes of PR-8 and the walked-tile record of D-567 read four directions alone. |
| D-717 | 2026-09-20 | The camera limits (OQ-118) | Game computes the camera position on each tick. It follows the lead, it clamps to the edges of a map larger than the view, and it centers a map smaller than the view. The limits and the position smoothing of the Godot camera stay off. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Closes OQ-118. Applies D-106 and F-52. One code path covers both cases, and a test reads the position with no engine. The rule rests on no undocumented behavior of the engine, which F-52 records. The clamp and the centering each take a test. |
| D-718 | 2026-09-20 | The shape of the sight of a patrol (OQ-114) | A patrol sees the quarter of the map that it faces, out to its range. It also notices any of the eight tiles that touch it. A wall stops the sight in each case. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Answers the patrol part of OQ-114. Applies D-37, D-207, and D-265, and it applies the effect line of D-208: the facing carries the sight. The facing of each patrol already enters the snapshot for the three views of D-207 and for the approach rule of D-265, so the record grows by nothing. The quadrant leaves three safe quarters, which makes the sneak of D-265 a repeatable action. The ring of one tile stops a walk along the flank of a patrol. The rule needs no division, so Core keeps integer math (T-7). PR-8 builds the patrol half. |
| D-719 | 2026-09-20 | The sight of the party (OQ-114) | The party sees every direction out to its range, and a wall stops it. An enemy or a thing outside that range is not drawn, and the ground under it is drawn. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Answers the party part of OQ-114. Applies D-37, D-566, D-193, and D-442. The party takes no facing for the sight, so the player never turns the lead to look. One range number carries the time of day: a day map reaches past the view, and a night map hides a patrol until it is close. This gives a night map its threat with no fog of war, which D-566 removed. Revised in part by D-814 on 2026-09-21: no range and no wall of the party hides an enemy or a thing. The range of each time of day stands as the ceiling of D-720. |
| D-720 | 2026-09-20 | The range floor of a patrol (OQ-114) | A map gives no patrol a longer sight range than the party has on that map. A load that breaks the rule fails with the map and the id of the enemy. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Answers the fairness part of OQ-114, and closes OQ-114 with D-718 and D-719. Applies T-2. The player never loses to a thing that it could not see, so the challenge comes from the layout and the timing. The check at load holds the rule, and no content author can break it by accident. PR-8 adds the check for each enemy that a map places. |
| D-721 | 2026-09-20 | The map HUD of PR-7 | PR-7 builds no map HUD. The health mark and the status mark move to PR-64, which already owns the status mark and which follows the PR that gives a character health. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-212, D-390, and G-16. This build holds no party, no health, and no status, so a HUD here would draw a value that no rule holds (T-2). PR-64 owns the whole map HUD, so one PR builds it and one set of exit tests covers it (G-8). The roadmap of PR-7 and of PR-64 changes with this decision. |
| D-722 | 2026-09-20 | The demo panel of PR-61 | The map takes the place of the demo panel of PR-61, and the panel goes. The row of button prompts stays at the bottom edge of the frame. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-222, D-561, and T-1: no dead code stays after the map takes the place of the panel, and the player still reads which button confirms and which opens the menu. PR-33 writes the title screen that it needs, with no demo panel to unpick. The layout test of the panel takes the prompt bar as its subject. Revised in part by D-815 on 2026-09-21: the row of prompts goes. The removal of the demo panel stands. |
| D-723 | 2026-09-20 | How a development build reaches the console (OQ-210) | The Game project references the debug assembly in every configuration except `ExportRelease`, and Game names no type of it. One file, `DebugSeam`, loads the assembly by name and reads each entry member as a delegate. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-210. Applies D-171, D-260, D-492, F-27, and T-2: no conditional compilation enters Game or Core, and a development build that cannot load the assembly fails with the assembly, the type, and the member. This row is the decision entry of the `GodotSharp` package at 4.7.2, which the debug project takes for the engine types of the console (G-13). The debug project takes no Godot source generator, so no type of it derives from a Godot node and the console builds engine nodes and connects to their signals. A test reads the names of the seam from the built Game assembly and finds each member in the built debug assembly. Conditional compilation in Game, and a filter of the export, were the other options. |
| D-724 | 2026-09-20 | The commands of the first console (OQ-211) | Four commands: `reveal` marks every tile of the map as walked and sends a debug intent, and `hash`, `where`, and `help` report and send none. A command that changes no state makes no intent. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-211. Applies D-171, D-493, D-567, and T-7: an intent carries an id and no value, so no command takes an argument, and a line of more than one word is a fault that names what the session read. One cheat proves the seam from the console to the record to a replay, and the record holds no line that no rule reads. Each later PR that gives the rules a new value adds its own commands. Every command makes an intent, the two smallest sets, and the four walk commands were the other options. |
| D-725 | 2026-09-20 | The console key and the world (OQ-212) | The backquote key opens the console and closes it, and the world keeps its ticks. The entry of the console takes every key, and the game makes no intent at all while the console is open. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-212. Applies D-162, D-214, D-650, F-50, and T-2: the key sits outside the input map, so it makes no intent and the remap of PR-63 never reaches it; the menu state keeps one meaning for PR-62; and the held set of the step keys empties on each change, so no party walks on while the person types. The host reads the key before every node of the frame and marks the event as handled, because the entry of an open console would take it first. A pause through the menu intent, and the F1 key, were the other options. |
| D-726 | 2026-09-20 | The proof of a release export (OQ-213) | Two checks. A test reads the project file of Game and fails when the reference of the debug assembly carries no `ExportRelease` condition, and a step of the export job searches each exported build for a file of that assembly. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-213. Applies D-449, D-492, D-512, and T-2: the fast check runs on every pull request, and the slow one reads the real export on each of the three systems. The export step also reads the Game assembly of the export first, because a search of the wrong folder finds no file of either name. The smoke session of a release export reports an absent console, and the job reads that line too. The export step alone, and a test that builds the `ExportRelease` configuration, were the other options. |
| D-727 | 2026-09-20 | The kind of a debug intent id (OQ-214) | The kind is `debug`, as in `debug.reveal_map`. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-214. Applies D-166, D-171, and D-646: the kind names the source of the entry, which is the debug assembly, and the seam test of PR-6 already used that kind. An id is permanent, so the answer binds every later debug command. The action field of a run record thus holds two kinds, `intent` and `debug`, and the debug mark of the record says the same. The kind `intent` with a `debug_` name was the other option. |
| D-728 | 2026-09-20 | The stale file of a deleted script (OQ-215) | PR-45 deletes `TheThingBelow.Game/scripts/Ui/BaseScreen.cs.uid`, which names a script that PR #42 deleted. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-215. Applies D-722, G-8, and T-1: the file is one line of the tree that a merge left wrong, and this pull request corrects the stale names of that script. The Godot editor writes such a file for each script, and it writes none for a script that no longer exists. |
| D-729 | 2026-09-20 | How the screen-test job pins Mesa (OQ-79) | The job installs Mesa from one pinned timestamp of `snapshot.ubuntu.com`, and a step fails when an installed version differs from the pinned one. No container and no source build. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-79, which blocked PR-41. Applies D-172 and T-7. The register gave the archive of the system as the fault of a package install, because it drops an old version. The snapshot service removes that fault: it serves the Noble indexes at a fixed timestamp, and a probe of 2026-09-20 read `https://snapshot.ubuntu.com/ubuntu/20260901T000000Z/dists/noble/Release` with the code 200. D-730 holds the pin. A container image with a fixed digest, a public image by digest, and a build from a source tag were the other options. The hosted runner image is not pinned, so a GitHub image update can still change a preinstalled library. |
| D-730 | 2026-09-20 | The Mesa pin of the screen-test job | The pin is `25.2.8-0ubuntu0.24.04.2`, from the snapshot `20260901T000000Z` of Ubuntu Noble. The job installs `mesa-vulkan-drivers`, `libgl1-mesa-dri`, and `libvulkan1` from that snapshot. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. This row is the decision entry of Mesa under G-13. Applies D-729 and D-731. A probe of 2026-09-20 read the version of `libgl1-mesa-dri` and of `mesa-vulkan-drivers` from `noble-updates` at that timestamp, and both gave `25.2.8-0ubuntu0.24.04.2`. Mesa carries the MIT license. A move of the pin is a PR, and that PR writes the new baseline too, because a Mesa update can change a pixel (D-172). |
| D-731 | 2026-09-20 | The renderer of the screen-test job | The job runs the Mobile renderer on lavapipe, the software Vulkan driver of the pinned Mesa. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Revises D-172 in part, the renderer of the CI image alone: the Compatibility renderer becomes the Mobile renderer. The two baselines, the pinned Mesa, and the contact sheet of D-172 stand. Applies D-616, which picked Mobile for every shipped build five days after D-172. Thus each capture shows the renderer that every player gets, and the light, the glow, and the particles of PR-56 to PR-60 meet a test of the right picture (D-188). Section 7.13 of `docs/roadmaps/area-effects.md` and section 7.12 of `docs/roadmaps/area-ci.md` follow. The Compatibility renderer on llvmpipe, and both renderers, were the other options. |
| D-732 | 2026-09-20 | How Game writes a capture | A `--capture <folder>` argument of Game, beside `--smoke`. One session builds each fixture, sets the window size and the fit of each capture, and writes one PNG for each one. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-172, D-232, D-568, and T-1: the argument path and the fixed-step loop of `TheThingBelow.Game/scripts/Boot.cs` already exist, and one session writes every capture. Godot Movie Maker, which D-551 gives to PR-74, and one scene file for each capture were the other options. A scene file would hold text and settings in a `.tscn`, against D-116 and D-499. |
| D-733 | 2026-09-20 | How a PR makes a new baseline | Every run of the screen-test job uploads each captured PNG as an artifact. When a change of a screen is intended, the author downloads the artifact, reads each frame, and commits it as the new baseline. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-172, D-511, and T-2. A baseline comes from the renderer of CI, and the Mac of the owner is Apple silicon, so no local run reproduces it. CI keeps read access alone, and a person reads every new frame before it becomes the truth. A `workflow_dispatch` job with write access, and a local run under an emulated x86_64 container, were the other options. |
| D-734 | 2026-09-20 | The fixtures of the screen-test job | Two fixtures. The `map` fixture is the running screen: the frame, the world, and the row of button prompts. The `ui` fixture shows the window frame and the longest string of the string table, with no world. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-172, D-241, D-708, and D-722, which removed the demo panel of PR-61. Exit test 1 and exit test 2 of PR-41 meet the `map` fixture, because the frame of PR-61 holds the map scene of PR-7. The `ui` fixture catches a change of the window frame or of the text that the map hides, and PR-62 and PR-63 add their screens to the same list. The `map` fixture alone, and a third fixture of the crash screen, were the other options. Revised in part by D-815 on 2026-09-21: the `map` fixture shows no row of prompts. The two fixtures stand. |
| D-735 | 2026-09-20 | The contact sheet of the screen tests | A local Godot run captures each fixture with the real renderer on the Mac, and the `screens` command of Tools joins the captures into one sheet under `artifacts/`. No sheet enters git, and `gh --attach` puts it in a PR description. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-172, D-514, and D-668: no review image enters git, and the PR description names each capture in order, because a sheet holds no text. A sheet in git, and no sheet file at all, were the other options. |
| D-736 | 2026-09-20 | Where the baselines live | `screens/baseline/` at the root of the checkout holds one committed PNG for each capture. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-172 and D-508. The project file of Game carries `content/` into every export, so a baseline there would ship to each player. A folder under `TheThingBelow.Tests/` was the other option, and the `screens` command of Tools reads the baselines in CI, not a test. |

## The enemies on the map

| Id | Date | Topic | Decision | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D-737 | 2026-09-20 | How a large enemy holds its tiles (OQ-115) | The body blocks every tile that it holds. Its sort value comes from its front row, and its sight starts at the tile of the body nearest the party. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-115, which blocked PR-8. Applies D-206, D-209, D-718, D-719, and D-720. A 2 by 2 body holds no center tile, so no rule needs a pick for an even size. The body sees where it blocks, so the range floor of D-720 reads from the edge of the body, and the player reads the whole body as the threat. Section 7.3 of `docs/roadmaps/area-exploration.md` follows. The front row for all three, and the center tile for all three, were the other options. |
| D-738 | 2026-09-20 | Where the record of a map enemy lives | A second array, `enemies`, in the map file of D-528, beside `things`. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-495, D-528, D-535, T-1, and T-2. One rule file still holds each place for the author, the review, and the content hash. The fields of an enemy stay off the thing record, so no other kind needs a refusal of a field that it never reads, as `pickable` needs today. A kind in `things`, and a placement file beside the map file, were the other options. |
| D-739 | 2026-09-20 | The form of a patrol route | A list of tiles. Each leg is straight and on one axis, and the patrol walks to the last tile and then back down the list. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-37, D-716, and T-1. The load refuses a leg that bends, a leg that leaves the map, and a leg that crosses a tile that takes no step. The author reads the path from the file, and a corridor patrol takes two tiles. Exit test 1 of PR-8 reads this rule. A list that closes into a loop, and a list of steps with a count each, were the other options. A corridor patrol has no circuit, so the loop form cannot write the common case. |
| D-740 | 2026-09-20 | A fixed enemy and a patrol | One record serves both. A route of one tile stands still, and the facing of the record gives its sight. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-37, D-207, and T-1. One walk rule and one sight rule serve both cases, so no branch exists to get wrong. Game reads the count of the route tiles to pick the flip of a standing enemy or the three views of a moving one. A `kind` field with the values fixed and patrol was the other option. |
| D-741 | 2026-09-20 | The area of a large enemy | The record gives a rectangle, and the enemy steps at random inside it. The load proves that the body fits at every anchor tile of the rectangle. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-206, D-209, G-4, and T-2. The enemy draws its direction from the exploration stream, and it keeps that direction while the step holds the body inside the area. Thus a record holds a route or an area, and never both. Exit test 4 of PR-8 reads the fit. A rectangle with a route inside it, and a body that turns in place, were the other options. D-209 already refused the route form, and it also refused a body that stands perfectly still. |
| D-742 | 2026-09-20 | The step of an enemy | Each record gives the count of ticks of one step. The load refuses a value under the party step of `MapRules`, so no enemy outwalks the party. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-164, D-203, D-660, and D-720. The party steps in 15 ticks, and the fixture enemies take 30, which is two steps each second. Content makes a slow boss or a quick scout with no code change, and PR-30 tunes each number. One constant for every enemy, and a field with no floor, were the other options. An enemy faster than the party cannot be walked away from, which breaks the fairness line of D-720. Revised in part by D-821 on 2026-09-21: the party steps in 16 ticks, and an enemy takes 16, 32, or 64. The floor of the party step stands. |
| D-743 | 2026-09-20 | The routes and the time of day | A record holds a list of routes, and each route names the times of day that pick it. A time that no route names keeps the enemy off the map at that time. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-193, D-442, D-555, and T-2. One field carries both clauses of D-193: the route by the time, and which enemies appear. A killed enemy keeps one id across a change of the time, so a story flag never brings it back. The load refuses a record that names one time two times, and one that names no time at all. Exit test 3 of PR-8 reads this rule. A `times` list with one route each, and one route for every time, were the other options. |
| D-744 | 2026-09-20 | The art of PR-8 | PR-8 ships one drawing for the fixture enemy, a front view. The three views and the two-frame walk of a moving enemy move to PR-17. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-107, D-207, D-519, G-8, and G-24. An art batch needs the generator of D-686 and an owner approval of its sheets, which is a second concern in a rules PR. Game draws the one view for every facing until PR-17 draws each enemy of the first playable. Section 7.6 of `docs/roadmaps/phase-2-first-playable.md`, section 7.6 of `docs/roadmaps/area-exploration.md`, and section 7.9 of `docs/roadmaps/area-art.md` follow. Three views with the walk frames, and a borrowed cast drawing, were the other options. A cast drawing would name an enemy id, against D-519. |
| D-745 | 2026-09-20 | The beat of the sight mark | A constant of `MapRules` counts the beat at 30 ticks, which is half a second. Nothing cancels the beat. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-208, D-522, and T-7. The beat replays from the seed with no intent, so a bot and a player meet the same map. Game draws the mark over exactly those ticks. Avoidance stays a matter of no sight at all, and the mark is the feedback that D-208 asks for. A wait intent from Game, and a beat that the party breaks with the sight, were the other options. A bot sends a wait intent at once, so the first option costs a bot nothing. Revised in part by D-1094 on 2026-09-25: a beat that ends while the lead walks waits for the end of that step. The count of 30 ticks and the rule that nothing cancels the beat stand. |
| D-746 | 2026-09-20 | Which side reached the other from behind | The facing of each side against the tile of the other. A patrol ambushes the party when it stands in the quarter behind the facing of the party, and the party sneaks a patrol on the same rule. When both hold, or neither holds, no side acts first. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-265, D-718, and T-1. One function reads a facing and two tiles, and it serves both sides with the quadrant math of D-718. The map state records the side at the start of the encounter, and PR-9 reads it. The side that started the encounter, and the direction of the last step of each side, were the other options. A standing enemy never steps, so the last-step rule leaves a fixed enemy with no ambush. |
| D-747 | 2026-09-20 | The block of an enemy and the step into it | Every tile of the body refuses a step. A step into it starts the encounter at once, with no beat, and the party is the side that reached the other. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-206, D-209, D-265, and D-493. This is the sneak of D-265 in the hands of the player, and the beat of D-208 stays the telegraph of the patrol. A block with no answer to the step, and a body that blocks nothing, were the other options. The first leaves the sneak of D-265 with no path, and the second empties the tiles of D-206 of meaning. Revised in part by D-1085 on 2026-09-25: during the grace time of D-381, a step into the body starts no encounter. The block of the body stands. Revised in part by D-1104 on 2026-09-25: a step into the enemy whose mark runs takes the side of the facings. The block and the start of the encounter at once stand. |
| D-748 | 2026-09-20 | The grace time after a flee | 300 ticks, which is 5 seconds, as a constant of `MapRules`. The map state counts it against the enemy record that the party fled from. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-381, D-531, and T-7. Revises OQ-133 in part: the grace time number moves to this row, and OQ-133 keeps the flee chance for PR-9. Five seconds walks the party 20 tiles, which clears the longest party sight range of 12 on any map. Exit test 5 of PR-8 reads this rule. A count against the group id, and 600 ticks, were the other options. A count against the group makes a whole wing of a dungeon safe after one flee. |
| D-749 | 2026-09-20 | What ends an encounter in PR-8 | The map state holds the encounter: the enemy id, the group id, and the side that reached the other from behind. No map system ticks while it holds. The console of PR-45 gains one command that ends it as a flee. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-265, D-381, D-531, D-724, and D-727. Exit test 5 of PR-8 gains a live path, and the smoke session and each bot run never stop. PR-9 replaces that command with the fight. A state that nothing ends, and a beat that ends into the grace time with no state, were the other options. The first stops the smoke session at the first sight, and the second records no side, against D-265. Revised in part by D-767 on 2026-09-20, the command: the fight of PR-9 ends the encounter, and the flee command leaves. The encounter state stands. |
| D-750 | 2026-09-20 | The enemies in the snapshot | Save format 3. The snapshot holds, for each enemy of the map, its id, its tile, its facing, the leg of its route, the ticks of its step, its grace time, and whether it is dead. The encounter state sits beside it. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-166, D-259, D-555, D-654, and G-5. A load puts each patrol back where it stood, so a save and a reload never move a fight, and a killed enemy stays dead. PR-8 keeps the reader of format 2 and commits a fixture save of format 3. The dead set alone, and no enemy state at all, were the other options. With no enemy state, a load rebuilds each patrol from content, and the state hash after the load differs from the hash before it. Revised in part by D-1111 on 2026-09-25: a save of another build matches each enemy by its id. A save of this build keeps each rule of this row. |
| D-751 | 2026-09-20 | What a step of the enemies logs | A sight that starts a beat, an encounter that starts, and a flee that starts a grace time each log at the info level. Each step of each enemy logs at the debug level. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-179 and D-660. The log file of a player holds the events that a report follows, and not the walk, so a crash report names the last encounter. Every line at the debug level, and no line for a step, were the other options. D-660 named the step of a patrol as the case for the debug level. |
| D-752 | 2026-09-20 | The id kind of a map enemy | The kind `patrol`, as in `patrol.fixture_dungeon_hall`. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-12, D-166, D-646, and D-740. One record covers a fixed enemy and a walking one, so one word names both, and the roadmaps already call a map enemy a patrol. The enemy record of PR-80 keeps the kind `enemy`, so one kind never names two record types. The glossary of the `ste-writing` skill gains the term. The kind `post`, and the kind `enemy` shared with PR-80, were the other options. |
| D-753 | 2026-09-20 | The group id of a map enemy | The record holds a `group` field, and the load of PR-8 checks its form alone. The test that proves each named group exists lands with the group file in PR-11. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-166, D-535, D-646, G-16, and T-2. PR-9 reads the group from the map to start a fight, and PR-9 lands before PR-11, so the field belongs in this PR. The content then needs no rewrite. A field that PR-11 adds was the other option, and it moves PR-9 or PR-11 in the order. Revised in part by D-766 on 2026-09-20, the PR of the existence test: PR-9 holds it. The `group` field stands. |
| D-754 | 2026-09-20 | The size of an enemy in content | The map record gives the size of the body. When PR-80 lands, its enemy record gives the size too, and the load fails a map whose size disagrees with the record. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-206, D-209, D-236, D-557, and T-2. The load of PR-8 needs the size for the body fit and for the block, and the layout author reads the body size in the file that holds the layout. One value then sits in two files, with a check that keeps them equal. A field that PR-80 removes, and a size from the group file of PR-11, were the other options. |

## The battle core

The owner answered the open questions of PR-9 and the gaps that the start of PR-9 found, on 2026-09-20. Each answer is one row.

| Id | Date | Topic | Decision | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D-755 | 2026-09-20 | A defend action (OQ-124) | A character can defend. A defend cuts the damage that the character takes until the next turn of that character. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-124. Applies D-35 and D-376: a hurt character with no heal and no item still has a useful turn. D-777 sets the cut and the delay. No defend action was the other option. |
| D-756 | 2026-09-20 | The turns that the strip shows (OQ-125) | Six. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-125. Applies D-29, D-376, and D-568: six faces of 32 pixels fit the frame, and the player reads about two rounds ahead. Core gives the next six turns, and PR-10 draws them. Twelve was the other option. |
| D-757 | 2026-09-20 | Where the delay of an action lives (OQ-126) | In content. Each action and each item holds its delay, and a test proves that every action has one. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-126. Applies D-116, G-6, and G-14: PR-30 tunes each delay with no code change. A battle rules file holds the delay of each action of PR-9. A table in code was the other option. |
| D-758 | 2026-09-20 | A group larger than its rows (OQ-132) | The extra enemies wait off the field, and each one steps in when a place opens. A fight can hold a wave. The session recommended a load failure, and the owner chose the wave. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-132. Applies D-31, D-377, and D-535. The timeline and the rows gain the wave case and its tests in PR-9. D-759 to D-762 set the rules of the wave. |
| D-759 | 2026-09-20 | The places of each row | The field holds six enemies in total, in any split of the front row and the back row. No row has a limit of its own. The session recommended three in each row, and the owner chose any split. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-31, D-377, and D-758. A waiting enemy steps in when fewer than six enemies stand. PR-10 draws a row of up to six enemies. Revised in part by D-778 on 2026-09-20, the trigger: each fall starts the step of the next waiting enemy. The limit of six and the free split stand. |
| D-760 | 2026-09-20 | The row of a waiting enemy | Each entry of a group names its row. A waiting enemy steps into that row, in the order of the group. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-535, D-758, and D-759. Content designs each wave. The slot of the fallen enemy, and the back row always, were the other options. |
| D-761 | 2026-09-20 | The first turn of a waiting enemy | The enemy joins the timeline at the tick that it steps in, one attack push out at its own speed. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-376 and D-758. A kill never gives an enemy a free turn, and the strip shows the new enemy before it acts. A turn at once was the other option. |
| D-762 | 2026-09-20 | The size limit of a group | Twelve enemies, the waiting ones included. The load fails a larger group, and the error names the group and the count. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-758 and T-2. A fight holds at most two full fields, and a content error never makes a fight of fifty. No limit was the other option. |
| D-763 | 2026-09-20 | The flee chance (OQ-133) | A base chance in content, plus a term for the average speed of the party against the average speed of the enemies on the field, clamped to a floor and a ceiling in basis points. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Resolves OQ-133 with D-748, which set the grace time. Applies D-169 and D-378. The roll draws on the battle stream. D-777 sets the numbers. A fixed chance for each kind of encounter was the other option. |
| D-764 | 2026-09-20 | The target of a battle intent | An intent gains an optional target: the side and the slot. The record writes it as one more field, so the record format goes from 1 to 2. A record replays on its own simulation version alone (G-17), so a record of format 1 fails with its line and needs no reader. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Revises D-493 in part, the form of an intent: an intent can carry a target. The rest of D-493 stands. Applies D-166 and T-7. Intents that move a cursor in Core were the other option, and they put each cursor move in the record. |
| D-765 | 2026-09-20 | The party stats before PR-67 | A rule fixture file holds each character: the health, the attack, the defense, the speed, and the start row. The run starts with Marrek alone (D-336). The snapshot keeps the health and the down of each character, so the save format goes from 3 to 4. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-36, D-166, D-537, and G-6. PR-67 replaces the fixed stats with the stat curves. Constants in code were the other option. |

The `group` field of D-753 stands. Applies D-535 and D-557: PR-11 grows the file into the group file of each region, with the same ids. One fixed group for every encounter was the other option. |

Revises D-749 in part, the command: the fight ends the encounter, and no command ends it as a flee. The encounter state of D-749 stands. Applies D-532 and D-724: a command sends its intent with the debug mark. A development build alone can end a fight until PR-10. A placeholder panel, and a fight that plays itself, were the other options. |
| D-768 | 2026-09-20 | The push of an action | The push is the delay of the action times 100, divided by the speed of the user, in whole ticks and at least 1. Haste and slow multiply the push in basis points. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-169, D-376, and G-2. Double speed halves the wait, and no speed gives a push of zero. The delay minus the speed was the other option, and it breaks at a high speed. |
| D-769 | 2026-09-20 | Two combatants ready on one tick | The higher speed acts first, then the party before the enemies, then the lower slot. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-376 and T-7. The order stays the same on every machine and spends no roll. The party first alone, and a draw from the battle stream, were the other options. |
| D-770 | 2026-09-20 | The start of a fight | Each combatant starts one attack push out, at its own speed. Each member of the side that came from behind starts at tick 0, so that whole side acts first, in the order of D-769. With no side behind, speed alone sets the first turn. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-265, D-746, and D-376. An ambush and a sneak each give a real swing. One free turn for the fastest member was the other option. |
| D-771 | 2026-09-20 | The form of the damage | The damage is the attack times the power of the action in basis points, divided by 10000, times 100, divided by 100 plus the defense, and at least 1. | D-772 | 2026-09-20 | The roll of a hit | Each hit draws a factor from 9000 to 11000 basis points on the battle stream. An attack can miss, with a low chance. No hit is critical in PR-9. The session recommended the factor with no miss, and the owner added a low miss chance. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-169, D-376, and G-4. D-773 sets what the miss chance reads. Exact damage was the other option. |
| D-773 | 2026-09-20 | What sets the miss chance | A base chance, plus a term for the speed of the target against the speed of the attacker, clamped to a floor and a ceiling. The same rule serves both sides. The session recommended one flat chance, and the owner chose the speed gap. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-376 and D-772. A fast target is harder to hit, and the ceiling keeps every combatant in reach. D-777 sets the numbers. PR-66 can raise the chance with a status. |
| D-774 | 2026-09-20 | The turn of a fixture enemy | A basic attack on a legal target that the battle stream draws. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. Applies D-65, D-377, and D-557. The evaluator of PR-11 replaces the draw. The first legal target was the other option. |
| D-775 | 2026-09-20 | The item action before PR-13 | A fixture file holds one healing draught and its start count in the pack. A battle rate in basis points cuts its effect in a fight. | D-777 | 2026-09-20 | The starting numbers of the fight | The delays, in ticks at speed 100: attack 100, item 100, flee 100, defend 60, row step 60, and a heavy blow of the tests 160. Haste is 7500 and slow is 15000 on each push, and a stun adds 50 ticks. The flee chance is 5000, plus 100 for each point of the speed gap, clamped 1000 to 9000. A draught heals 30 at a battle rate of 5000, and the pack holds 3. Marrek has health 60, attack 12, defense 4, and speed 100. A grunt has health 30, attack 8, defense 2, and speed 90. A brute has health 80, attack 14, defense 6, and speed 80. The group `group.fixture_pair` holds two grunts in front. The group `group.fixture_elite` holds a brute in front, two grunts behind, and two grunts that wait. No fixture group is a boss. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-20. ## The gitar pause

The gitar subscription expires. On 2026-09-22 the owner paused the gitar pass until the owner says that gitar is back. One row holds the pause, and a later row ends it.

| Id | Date | Topic | Decision | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D-895 | 2026-09-22 | The gitar pause | The gitar pass stops. While the pause holds, no PR waits for a gitar pass or answers one, and the PR gate line of the pass holds. A session applies the `review-override` label to a docs PR that changes no decision row after the ste-check job is green on the head. The review-gate check then reads the label. When the owner says that gitar is back, one PR ends the pause with the steps of `docs/runbooks/session-context.md`. The owner chose each part from the options of the session. | Owner answer, 2026-09-22. Revises D-14, D-66, D-67, D-586, D-587, and D-705 in part for the time of the pause, and each row names its part. T-4, D-16, D-401, and the review of the other provider stand. No branch protection rule, no workflow, and no review-gate rule reads a gitar result, so none of them changes. Each paused rule outside the dated records cites this row. Superseded by D-909 on 2026-09-23: the pause ends. |
| D-896 | 2026-09-22 | The place of the procedural fog (OQ-220) | The procedural fog is PR-94, and it comes before PR-59, the glow. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-22. PR-94 after PR-60 was the other option. |

| D-926 | 2026-09-23 | The automated review of the other provider | `make codex-review PR=<n>` starts the review of the other provider through the Codex CLI. The Makefile target is thin, and the `codex-review` command of Tools holds the logic and its tests. The command passes the model `gpt-6-luna`, the reasoning effort `medium`, the sandbox `danger-full-access`, and the approval policy `never` on the command line, so no value comes from the configuration file of the CLI. It refuses a run when the CLI is absent or older than the pinned minimum, the model probe fails, the PR is not open, the checkout differs from origin, the working tree has changes, or the Gitar pass of the effective head is not complete. It runs the review in a separate git worktree at the head of the PR, on a local branch that tracks the PR branch, so the author checkout does not change. The prompt is `Review PR #<n>.`, with an instruction to load the `pr-review` skill and an instruction to push the record and the handoff entry as one metadata commit with `HEAD:<branch>`. After the run, the command fetches, reads the record on origin, compares its head field with the effective head (D-610), and prints the verdict, the open finding ids, and the path of the transcript. The exit codes: 0 for an approval, 2 for `Changes required` or `Blocked`, 3 for the three-strike stop, and 1 for a fault or a refusal. | Owner direction, 2026-09-23: "automating Codex reviews via a make codex-review command. We will use GPT-6 Luna on Medium." The owner chose the sandbox on 2026-09-23, as the session recommended, because the reviewer needs the network, the dotnet caches, and the git folder of the main checkout. A probe on 2026-09-23 ran the model at that effort with CLI 0.156.1. The command automates the start of the review alone: the depth of the review and the form of the record stay the same. Revises D-576 in part: the author session starts the reviewer as a separate Codex process, and that process is a clean reviewer session of its own, bound to the same PR. Revised in part by D-946 on 2026-09-23: the flag `--skip-gitar-review` skips the refusal on a Gitar pass that is not complete. Each other part stands. Revised in part by D-1087 on 2026-09-25: each program of the command runs under a time limit, and a run can end at a limit. Each other part stands. |

## The evaluator and the waiting enemies

The owner answered the questions of PR-11 and OQ-242 at the start of PR-11, on 2026-09-23.

| Id | Date | Topic | Decision | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D-947 | 2026-09-23 | The tie-break of the evaluator (OQ-127) | When two legal actions have the same score, the evaluator draws one of them from its own seeded stream. The evaluator draws only on a tie. The session first recommended the first action in a fixed order. The owner asked why a draw from the stream is not the answer, and the session then recommended the draw from the stream of the evaluator. | Owner answer, 2026-09-23. Resolves OQ-127. Applies D-35, D-65, G-4, and T-7. The same seed and the same inputs give the same choice on every machine. A tie moves only the later rolls of the evaluator stream, and never a hit roll or a damage roll. A test of one choice of a tie sets its seed. |
| D-948 | 2026-09-23 | What makes a profile unable to act (OQ-128) | At load, the validator puts each enemy of each group in a check fight with its profile, against one fixture party member. The validator then builds the legal actions of the enemy. An empty list fails the load, and the error names the group, the enemy, and the profile. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-23. Resolves OQ-128. Applies D-65, G-21, and T-2. An enemy that can only heal an ally that it never has fails the load. The check costs load time for each enemy, and PR-11 reports that cost with the cost of a turn (F-53). A check of weights that are all zero was the other option. |
| D-949 | 2026-09-23 | The chance of a steal (OQ-129) | Each profile gives a base chance of a steal, in basis points. The Theft aptitude of the thief adds to that chance, and a clamp holds the sum. A failure costs the turn of the thief. The session found that an enemy has no level and that Core has no aptitude, and it recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-23. Resolves OQ-129. Applies D-45, D-281, and D-383. Content can make a guarded enemy hard to rob, and Theft still pays. The enemy record gets no level. PR-11 holds the base chance and its load check. PR-30 tunes the base chance and the Theft term. The Theft aptitude against a new level of the enemy, and a fixed chance, were the other options. |
| D-950 | 2026-09-23 | The PR of the steal action | PR-13 builds the steal action: the roll, the Theft term, the clamp, the failure that costs the turn, and the entry that goes into the pack. PR-11 holds the steal list and the base chance of each profile, with their load checks. The session recommended this. | Owner answer, 2026-09-23. Applies D-383, D-949, and G-8. No roadmap PR built the steal action. A stolen item needs the pack of PR-13, and PR-12 builds the aptitudes before PR-13. PR-12 and PR-11 were the other options. |
